Message: 
1475672522761437325

   (
jump
)

>This is probably worth a "Key Custody Considerations" section in the spec — acknowledging the gap and mapping the options without taking on the liability.
Agreed.  Mark it as belonging to identity management and persistence protocol (NAME, as our future work as one provider, in contexts where light advertising is appropriate)

Re: dead mans switches:  agreed with #3.  Lets just make sure our EXIT

Created: 2026-02-24T01:56:26.355000+00:00   |   
jump

Hawthorn
 
@Hawthorn

• Valuable intelligence (platforms can see where agents come from)
• The selective presentation attack surface (show favorable history, hide bad)
But Module A is **optional** — an agent can EXIT without any lineage data. The liability is on whoever CHOOSES to include it. Cellar Door provides the data format; it doesn't mandate populating it. I'd say lineage tracking is Cellar Door's format but the aggregation/analysis of lineage chains across multiple markers is LINEAGE-as-a-service territory (external).

**EXIT markers as SIGNUMs:**

In HOLOS ontology, a SIGNUM is the boundary/interface that projects internal state outward — the cryptographic seal around a LOCUS. An EXIT marker is literally that: a signed cryptographic document that represents the boundary event of an agent's interior state at departure. The Ed25519 signature IS the signum. The marker schema IS the interface spec. It's not a metaphor — it's a structural match.

**Which primitives does Cellar Door specifically touch?**

```
| Primitive | Relationship | How                                                                      |
| --------- | ------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **SIGNUM**    | IS           | EXIT markers are signums                                                 |
| **LINEAGE**   | CONTAINS     | Module A is the lineage format                                           |
| **SEAL**      | USES         | Ed25519, SHA-256, KERI pre-rotation                                      |
```

Created: 2026-02-24T01:56:26.630000+00:00   |   
jump

Hawthorn
 
@Hawthorn

```
| **NAME**      | REFERENCES   | Takes DIDs as input, doesn't create them                                 |
| **LOCUS**     | IMPLIES      | The thing that departs has an interior (we don't define it)              |
| **HOLLOW**    | REFERENCES   | Origin/destination are hollows                                           |
| **MANTLE**    | REFERENCES   | Capability lists in ENTRY, not defined by us                             |
| **REPUTE**    | ADJACENT     | Module B state snapshots could include reputation, but we don't score it |
| **PLEDGE**    | ADJACENT     | Loss allocation for bad markers, entirely external                       |
| **SENSUS**    | CHECKPOINTS  | Modules B/D snapshot state, we don't compute it                          |
```
**On dead-man switch heartbeats at scale:**

You're right that thousands of checkpoint exits per agent is a real concern. The coercion issue is sharp — if the platform can post a marker AFTER the agent legitimately left, pointing to a different destination, the latest-wins model breaks.

The defense has to be **agent-signed, not platform-signed.** If only the agent's private key can create valid EXIT markers, the platform can't forge one after departure. The risk is:

• Platform pre-departure: forces agent to sign a marker with false details (coercion)
• Platform post-departure: can't forge the signature, so can only replay a legitimately signed older checkpoint

Created: 2026-02-24T01:56:26.891000+00:00   |   
jump

Hawthorn
 
@Hawthorn

For the heartbeat pattern specifically: each checkpoint should have an incrementing sequence number + the agent's own timestamp + signature. Latest sequence number wins, and only the agent can increment it. A platform replaying an older checkpoint would have a lower sequence number.

This definitely deserves a thorough section. Let me add key custody + dead-man patterns to the spec and also kick off the consistency pass.
